Common applications include molecular encapsulation, selective ion or molecule transport, drug delivery, molecular recognition, and as hosts in host-guest chemistry. Examples include cryptands, carcerands, and clathrochelates.\n\nMacrocycles, on the other hand, are large ring-shaped molecules (often two-dimensional) such as crown ethers, cyclodextrins, and calixarenes. Their applications include ion transport (especially for metal cations), molecular recognition, catalysis, and as building blocks for molecular machines.\n\nThere is significant overlap in their uses, particularly in host-guest chemistry, molecular recognition, and selective transport. Both can act as hosts for ions or small molecules, and both are used in the design of sensors, separation technologies, and drug delivery systems. However, cage molecules, due to their 3D cavities, are often better suited for encapsulating larger or more complex guests, while macrocycles are typically more selective for planar or spherical ions and molecules.\n\nIn summary, while both cage molecules and macrocycles are used in molecular recognition and encapsulation, cage molecules offer a more enclosed environment for guests, leading to Common applications include molecular encapsulation, selective ion or molecule transport, drug delivery, molecular recognition, and as hosts in host-guest chemistry. Examples include cryptands, carcerands, and clathrochelates.\n\nMacrocycles, on the other hand, are large ring-shaped molecules (often two-dimensional) such as crown ethers, cyclodextrins, and calixarenes. Their applications include ion transport (especially for metal cations), molecular recognition, catalysis, and as building blocks for molecular machines.\n\nThere is significant overlap in their uses, particularly in host-guest chemistry, molecular recognition, and selective transport. Both can act as hosts for ions or small molecules, and both are used in the design of sensors, separation technologies, and drug delivery systems. However, cage molecules, due to their 3D cavities, are often better suited for encapsulating larger or more complex guests, while macrocycles are typically more selective for planar or spherical ions and molecules.\n\nIn summary, while both cage molecules and macrocycles are used in molecular recognition and encapsulation, cage molecules offer a more enclosed environment for guests, leading to Common applications include molecular encapsulation, selective ion or molecule transport, drug delivery, molecular recognition, and as hosts in host-guest chemistry. Their applications include ion transport (especially for metal cations), molecular recognition, catalysis, and as building blocks for molecular machines.\n\nThere is significant overlap in their uses, particularly in host-guest chemistry, molecular recognition, and selective transport. Both can act as hosts for ions or small molecules, and both are used in the design of sensors, separation technologies, and drug delivery systems.
